## dost1001-0932

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 1
- **Question:** A particle moves along a line with position $s(t)=t^{2}+2t$ meters. What is its instantaneous velocity at $t=3$ s?
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $5\ \text{m/s}$
  - **B:** $6\ \text{m/s}$
  - **C:** $15\ \text{m/s}$
  - **D:** $8\ \text{m/s}$
- **Answer Key:** **D** ($8\ \text{m/s}$)

**Explanation:**
Instantaneous velocity is the derivative of the position function with respect to time. Given $s(t)=t^2+2t$, we differentiate to find the velocity function $v(t)$: $v(t) = \frac{d}{dt}(t^2+2t) = 2t+2$. To find the instantaneous velocity at $t=3$ s, substitute $t=3$ into $v(t)$: $v(3) = 2(3)+2 = 6+2 = 8\ \text{m/s}$.

---

  - **C:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=\sin x$
  - **D:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=-\sin x$
- **Answer Key:** **B** ($\frac{dy}{dx}=\cos x$)

**Explanation:**
The derivative of the sine function is the cosine function. Therefore, if $y=\sin x$, then $\frac{dy}{dx}=\cos x$.

## dost1001-0934

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 1
- **Question:** Find $\int 6x^{2}\,dx$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $6x^{3}+C$
  - **B:** $3x^{2}+C$
  - **C:** $2x^{3}+C$
  - **D:** $2x^{2}+C$
- **Answer Key:** **C** ($2x^{3}+C$)

**Explanation:**
To find the indefinite integral of $6x^2$, we use the power rule for integration, which states that $\int x^n \,dx = \frac{x^{n+1}}{n+1} + C$. Applying this, we get $\int 6x^2 \,dx = 6 \cdot \frac{x^{2+1}}{2+1} + C = 6 \cdot \frac{x^3}{3} + C = 2x^3 + C$.

---

## dost1001-0935

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 1
- **Question:** Find $\int (3x-4)\,dx$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)x^{2}-4x+C$
  - **B:** $3x^{2}-4+C$
  - **C:** $\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)x^{2}-4+C$
  - **D:** $3x^{2}-4x+C$
- **Answer Key:** **A** ($\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)x^{2}-4x+C$)

**Explanation:**
To integrate the expression $(3x-4)$, we integrate each term separately using the power rule. For $3x$, we have $\int 3x^1 \,dx = 3 \cdot \frac{x^{1+1}}{1+1} + C_1 = \frac{3}{2}x^2 + C_1$. For the constant term $-4$, we have $\int -4 \,dx = -4x + C_2$. Combining these, the indefinite integral is $\frac{3}{2}x^2 - 4x + C$.

---

## dost1001-0936

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 1
- **Question:** Evaluate $\int_{0}^{2} x\,dx$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** 2
  - **B:** 1
  - **C:** 4
  - **D:** 8
- **Answer Key:** **A** (2)

**Explanation:**
To evaluate the definite integral $\int_{0}^{2} x\,dx$, we first find the antiderivative of $x$, which is $\frac{x^2}{2}$. Then, we apply the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus, evaluating the antiderivative at the upper limit and subtracting its value at the lower limit: $\left[\frac{x^2}{2}\right]_{0}^{2} = \frac{2^2}{2} - \frac{0^2}{2} = \frac{4}{2} - 0 = 2$.

---

## dost1001-0937

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 2
- **Question:** On the graph of a differentiable function, point P lies on an increasing segment, Q is a local maximum, R lies on a decreasing segment, and S is a local minimum. At which labeled point is $f'(x)>0$?
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** Q
  - **B:** R
  - **C:** S
  - **D:** P
- **Answer Key:** **D** (P)

**Explanation:**
The first derivative of a function, $f'(x)$, indicates the slope of the tangent line to the graph of $f(x)$. When $f'(x) > 0$, the function is increasing. Point P lies on an increasing segment, meaning its slope is positive, so $f'(x) > 0$ at P.

## dost1001-0938

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 2
- **Question:** Evaluate $\lim_{x\to 1}\frac{x^{2}-1}{x-1}$.
- **Image:** `dost1001-q0938.png`
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** 0
  - **B:** 1
  - **C:** Does not exist
  - **D:** 2
- **Answer Key:** **D** (2)

**Explanation:**
To evaluate $\lim_{x\to 1}\frac{x^{2}-1}{x-1}$, we first factor the numerator as a difference of squares: $x^2-1 = (x-1)(x+1)$. The expression becomes $\lim_{x\to 1}\frac{(x-1)(x+1)}{x-1}$. Since $x \to 1$, $x \neq 1$, so we can cancel the $(x-1)$ terms, leaving $\lim_{x\to 1}(x+1)$. Substituting $x=1$ into the simplified expression gives $1+1=2$.

---

## dost1001-0939

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 2
- **Question:** Evaluate $\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{e^{x}-1}{x}$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** 0
  - **B:** 1
  - **C:** $e$
  - **D:** Does not exist
- **Answer Key:** **B** (1)

**Explanation:**
This limit is a fundamental limit in calculus, often encountered when defining the derivative of $e^x$ at $x=0$. It can be evaluated using L'Hôpital's Rule since it is of the indeterminate form $\frac{0}{0}$: $\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{e^{x}-1}{x} = \lim_{x\to 0}\frac{\frac{d}{dx}(e^{x}-1)}{\frac{d}{dx}(x)} = \lim_{x\to 0}\frac{e^{x}}{1} = e^0 = 1$.

---

## dost1001-0940

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 2
- **Question:** A table gives $f(x)$ near $x=2$: $f(1.9)=3.91$, $f(1.99)=3.9901$, $f(2.01)=4.0101$, and $f(2.1)=\square$. What value is the table suggesting for $\lim_{x\to 2}f(x)$?
- **Image:** `dost1001-q0940.png`
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** 3
  - **B:** 4.2
  - **C:** 4
  - **D:** The limit cannot be estimated from a table.
- **Answer Key:** **C** (4)

**Explanation:**
The limit of a function as $x$ approaches a value is the value that the function approaches as $x$ gets arbitrarily close to that value from both the left and the right. From the table, as $x$ approaches $2$ from the left ($1.9, 1.99$), $f(x)$ approaches $4$ ($3.91, 3.9901$). As $x$ approaches $2$ from the right ($2.01$), $f(x)$ also approaches $4$ ($4.0101$). Therefore, the table suggests $\lim_{x\to 2}f(x) = 4$.

## dost1001-0941

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 2
- **Question:** Let $f(x)=\frac{x^{2}-4}{x-2}$ for $x\neq 2$ and $f(2)=k$. What value of $k$ makes $f$ continuous at $x=2$?
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** 4
  - **B:** 0
  - **C:** 2
  - **D:** Does not exist
- **Answer Key:** **A** (4)

**Explanation:**
For a function to be continuous at a point $x=c$, three conditions must be met: $f(c)$ must be defined, $\lim_{x\to c}f(x)$ must exist, and $\lim_{x\to c}f(x) = f(c)$. Here, $f(2)=k$. We need to find $\lim_{x\to 2}\frac{x^{2}-4}{x-2}$. Factoring the numerator, we get $\lim_{x\to 2}\frac{(x-2)(x+2)}{x-2} = \lim_{x\to 2}(x+2) = 2+2=4$. For continuity, $f(2)$ must equal this limit, so $k=4$.

---

## dost1001-0942

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 2
- **Question:** Differentiate $y=x^{2}(x+3)$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=2x(x+3)$
  - **B:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=3x^{2}+6x$
  - **C:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=x^{2}+6x$
  - **D:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=3x^{2}+3$
- **Answer Key:** **B** ($\frac{dy}{dx}=3x^{2}+6x$)

**Explanation:**
First, expand the function to $y = x^3 + 3x^2$. Then, apply the power rule for differentiation, $\frac{d}{dx}(x^n) = nx^{n-1}$, to each term: $\frac{dy}{dx} = 3x^{3-1} + 3(2)x^{2-1} = 3x^2 + 6x$.

---

## dost1001-0943

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 2
- **Question:** Differentiate $y=\frac{x+1}{x-1}$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=\frac{2}{x-1}^{2}$
  - **B:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=\frac{1}{x-1}^{2}$
  - **C:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=\frac{-2}{x+1}^{2}$
  - **D:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=\frac{-2}{x-1}^{2}$
- **Answer Key:** **D** ($\frac{dy}{dx}=\frac{-2}{x-1}^{2}$)

**Explanation:**
Apply the quotient rule for differentiation, $\frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{u}{v}\right) = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2}$, where $u = x+1$ and $v = x-1$. This gives $u' = 1$ and $v' = 1$, so $\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{(1)(x-1) - (x+1)(1)}{(x-1)^2} = \frac{x-1-x-1}{(x-1)^2} = \frac{-2}{(x-1)^2}$.

---

## dost1001-0944

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 2
- **Question:** Differentiate $y=(2x+1)^{4}$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=4(2x+1)^{3}$
  - **B:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=8(2x+1)^{3}$
  - **C:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=8(2x+1)^{4}$
  - **D:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=(2x+1)^{5}/5$
- **Answer Key:** **B** ($\frac{dy}{dx}=8(2x+1)^{3}$)

**Explanation:**
Apply the chain rule, $\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{dy}{du} \cdot \frac{du}{dx}$, where $u = 2x+1$ and $y = u^4$. Differentiating $y$ with respect to $u$ gives $\frac{dy}{du} = 4u^3$, and differentiating $u$ with respect to $x$ gives $\frac{du}{dx} = 2$. Multiplying these results yields $\frac{dy}{dx} = 4(2x+1)^3 \cdot 2 = 8(2x+1)^3$.

---

## dost1001-0945

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 2
- **Question:** If $f(x)=x^{3}-6x$, what is $f'(-1)$?
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $-9$
  - **B:** $-3$
  - **C:** 3
  - **D:** 9
- **Answer Key:** **B** ($-3$)

**Explanation:**
First, find the derivative of $f(x)$ using the power rule: $f'(x) = 3x^{3-1} - 6x^{1-1} = 3x^2 - 6$. Then, substitute $x = -1$ into the derivative: $f'(-1) = 3(-1)^2 - 6 = 3(1) - 6 = 3 - 6 = -3$.

---

## dost1001-0946

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 2
- **Question:** Find the tangent-line equation to $y=x^{2}+1$ at $x=2$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $y=2x+1$
  - **B:** $y=4x+5$
  - **C:** $y=2x-3$
  - **D:** $y=4x-3$
- **Answer Key:** **D** ($y=4x-3$)

**Explanation:**
First, find the $y$-coordinate at $x=2$: $y = (2)^2+1 = 5$. Next, find the derivative to get the slope: $\frac{dy}{dx} = 2x$. Evaluate the slope at $x=2$: $m = 2(2) = 4$. Finally, use the point-slope form $y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)$: $y - 5 = 4(x - 2)$, which simplifies to $y - 5 = 4x - 8$, or $y = 4x - 3$.

---

## dost1001-0947

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 2
- **Question:** The area of a circle is $A=\pi r^{2}$. If the radius increases at 2 cm/s, how fast is the area increasing when $r=3$ cm?
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $6\pi\ \text{cm}^{2}/\text{s}$
  - **B:** $9\pi\ \text{cm}^{2}/\text{s}$
  - **C:** $12\pi\ \text{cm}^{2}/\text{s}$
  - **D:** $18\pi\ \text{cm}^{2}/\text{s}$
- **Answer Key:** **C** ($12\pi\ \text{cm}^{2}/\text{s}$)

**Explanation:**
Differentiate the area formula $A=\pi r^2$ with respect to time $t$ using the chain rule: $\frac{dA}{dt} = \frac{dA}{dr} \cdot \frac{dr}{dt} = (2\pi r) \cdot \frac{dr}{dt}$. Given $\frac{dr}{dt} = 2$ cm/s and $r=3$ cm, substitute these values: $\frac{dA}{dt} = 2\pi (3 \text{ cm}) \cdot (2 \text{ cm/s}) = 12\pi \text{ cm}^2/\text{s}$.

---

## dost1001-0948

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 2
- **Question:** A rectangle has perimeter 40 m. What dimensions maximize its area?
- **Image:** `dost1001-q0948.png`
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** 5 m by 15 m
  - **B:** 8 m by 12 m
  - **C:** 10 m by 10 m
  - **D:** 4 m by 16 m
- **Answer Key:** **C** (10 m by 10 m)

**Explanation:**
Let the dimensions of the rectangle be $l$ and $w$. The perimeter is $2l + 2w = 40$, so $l + w = 20$, which means $w = 20 - l$. The area is $A = lw = l(20 - l) = 20l - l^2$. To maximize the area, find the derivative with respect to $l$ and set it to zero: $\frac{dA}{dl} = 20 - 2l = 0$, which gives $2l = 20$, so $l = 10$ m. Substituting $l=10$ back into $w = 20 - l$ gives $w = 10$ m, indicating a square maximizes the area.

---

## dost1001-0949

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 2
- **Question:** A particle has position $s(t)=t^{3}-6t^{2}+9t$ for $t\ge 0$. At what times is the particle at rest?
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $t=0$ s and $t=3$ s
  - **B:** $t=1$ s and $t=3$ s
  - **C:** $t=1$ s only
  - **D:** $t=3$ s only
- **Answer Key:** **B** ($t=1$ s and $t=3$ s)

**Explanation:**
A particle is at rest when its velocity is zero. The velocity function $v(t)$ is the derivative of the position function $s(t)$: $v(t) = s'(t) = 3t^2 - 12t + 9$. Set $v(t) = 0$ to find the times when the particle is at rest: $3t^2 - 12t + 9 = 0$. Divide by 3: $t^2 - 4t + 3 = 0$. Factor the quadratic equation: $(t-1)(t-3) = 0$. This yields $t=1$ s and $t=3$ s.

---

## dost1001-0950

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 2
- **Question:** For $f(x)=x^{3}-3x$, at what $x$-value does $f$ have a local minimum?
- **Image:** `dost1001-q0950.png`
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $x=-1$
  - **B:** $x=0$
  - **C:** $x=1$
  - **D:** $x=3$
- **Answer Key:** **C** ($x=1$)

**Explanation:**
To find local extrema, first find the derivative $f'(x) = 3x^2 - 3$. Set $f'(x) = 0$ to find critical points: $3x^2 - 3 = 0 \Rightarrow 3(x^2 - 1) = 0 \Rightarrow x = \pm 1$. Using the second derivative test, $f''(x) = 6x$. For $x=1$, $f''(1) = 6(1) = 6 > 0$, indicating a local minimum.

---

## dost1001-0951

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 2
- **Question:** Differentiate $y=\ln x$ for $x>0$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=\ln x$
  - **B:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=\frac{1}{x}$
  - **C:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=x$
  - **D:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=e^{x}$
- **Answer Key:** **B** ($\frac{dy}{dx}=\frac{1}{x}$)

**Explanation:**
The derivative of the natural logarithm function $y = \ln x$ with respect to $x$ is given by the fundamental differentiation rule $\frac{d}{dx}(\ln x) = \frac{1}{x}$. This rule applies directly for $x > 0$.

## dost1001-0952

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 2
- **Question:** Differentiate $y=e^{2x}$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=e^{2x}$
  - **B:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=2e^{x}$
  - **C:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=2e^{2x}$
  - **D:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=e^{x^{2}}$
- **Answer Key:** **C** ($\frac{dy}{dx}=2e^{2x}$)

**Explanation:**
To differentiate $y=e^{2x}$, we use the chain rule. The derivative of $e^u$ is $e^u \frac{du}{dx}$. Here, $u=2x$, so $\frac{du}{dx}=2$. Therefore, $\frac{dy}{dx} = e^{2x} \cdot 2 = 2e^{2x}$.

---

## dost1001-0953

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 2
- **Question:** Differentiate $y=\cos(3x)$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=3\sin(3x)$
  - **B:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=-3\sin(3x)$
  - **C:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=-\sin(3x)$
  - **D:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=3\cos(3x)$
- **Answer Key:** **B** ($\frac{dy}{dx}=-3\sin(3x)$)

**Explanation:**
To differentiate $y=\cos(3x)$, we apply the chain rule. The derivative of $\cos(u)$ is $-\sin(u) \frac{du}{dx}$. Here, $u=3x$, so $\frac{du}{dx}=3$. Thus, $\frac{dy}{dx} = -\sin(3x) \cdot 3 = -3\sin(3x)$.

---

## dost1001-0954

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 2
- **Question:** Find $\int (4x^{3}-2)\,dx$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $4x^{4}-2x+C$
  - **B:** $x^{4}-2+C$
  - **C:** $x^{3}-2x+C$
  - **D:** $x^{4}-2x+C$
- **Answer Key:** **D** ($x^{4}-2x+C$)

**Explanation:**
To find the indefinite integral of a polynomial, we apply the power rule for integration, $\int x^n \,dx = \frac{x^{n+1}}{n+1} + C$, and the constant rule, $\int k \,dx = kx + C$. For $\int (4x^3-2)\,dx$, we integrate term by term: $\int 4x^3\,dx = 4\frac{x^{3+1}}{3+1} = 4\frac{x^4}{4} = x^4$, and $\int -2\,dx = -2x$. Combining these, we get $x^4 - 2x + C$.

---

## dost1001-0955

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 2
- **Question:** Find $\int \sec^{2}x\,dx$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $\sec x+C$
  - **B:** $\cot x+C$
  - **C:** $\tan x+C$
  - **D:** $\sin x+C$
- **Answer Key:** **C** ($\tan x+C$)

**Explanation:**
The integral of $\sec^2 x$ is a standard trigonometric integral. The derivative of $\tan x$ is $\sec^2 x$, so by definition, the antiderivative of $\sec^2 x$ is $\tan x + C$.

## dost1001-0956

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 2
- **Question:** Find $\int e^{x}\,dx$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $xe^{x}+C$
  - **B:** $\ln x+C$
  - **C:** $e^{x}+C$
  - **D:** $e^{x+1}+C$
- **Answer Key:** **C** ($e^{x}+C$)

**Explanation:**
The integral of $e^x$ is a fundamental integration rule. The exponential function $e^x$ is its own derivative and its own integral. Therefore, $\int e^x\,dx = e^x + C$.

## dost1001-0957

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 2
- **Question:** Evaluate $\int_{0}^{3} 2\,dx$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** 2
  - **B:** 6
  - **C:** 3
  - **D:** 9
- **Answer Key:** **B** (6)

**Explanation:**
To evaluate the definite integral $\int_{0}^{3} 2\,dx$, we first find the antiderivative of the constant $2$, which is $2x$. Then, we apply the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus by evaluating the antiderivative at the upper limit and subtracting its value at the lower limit: $[2x]_{0}^{3} = 2(3) - 2(0) = 6 - 0 = 6$.

---

## dost1001-0958

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 2
- **Question:** Evaluate $\int_{0}^{\pi}\sin x\,dx$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** 0
  - **B:** 2
  - **C:** 1
  - **D:** $\pi$
- **Answer Key:** **B** (2)

**Explanation:**
To evaluate the definite integral, we find the antiderivative of $\sin x$, which is $-\cos x$. Then, we apply the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus by evaluating the antiderivative at the upper and lower limits and subtracting: $[-\cos x]_{0}^{\pi} = (-\cos \pi) - (-\cos 0) = (-(-1)) - (-1) = 1 + 1 = 2$.

---

## dost1001-0959

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 2
- **Question:** What is the area under $y=x^{2}$ from $x=0$ to $x=1$?
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $\frac{1}{2}$
  - **B:** 1
  - **C:** $\frac{2}{3}$
  - **D:** $\frac{1}{3}$
- **Answer Key:** **D** ($\frac{1}{3}$)

**Explanation:**
The area under the curve $y=x^2$ from $x=0$ to $x=1$ is given by the definite integral $\int_{0}^{1} x^2 \,dx$. Evaluating this integral, we get $[\frac{x^3}{3}]_{0}^{1} = \frac{1^3}{3} - \frac{0^3}{3} = \frac{1}{3} - 0 = \frac{1}{3}$.

---

## dost1001-0960

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 2
- **Question:** A particle has velocity $v(t)=3t^{2}$ m/s for $0\le t\le 2$. What is its displacement over this interval?
- **Image:** `dost1001-q0960.png`
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** 4 m
  - **B:** 6 m
  - **C:** 12 m
  - **D:** 8 m
- **Answer Key:** **D** (8 m)

**Explanation:**
Displacement is the definite integral of velocity with respect to time over the given interval. Thus, we evaluate $\int_{0}^{2} 3t^2 \,dt$. The antiderivative of $3t^2$ is $t^3$, so we have $[t^3]_{0}^{2} = 2^3 - 0^3 = 8 - 0 = 8$ meters.

---

## dost1001-0961

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** A graph of $f'(x)$ lies above the $x$-axis on $(-2,1)$, below on $(1,3)$, and above again on $(3,5)$. On which intervals is $f$ increasing?
- **Image:** `dost1001-q0961.png`
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $(-2,1)$ and $(3,5)$
  - **B:** $(1,3)$ only
  - **C:** $(-2,3)$ only
  - **D:** $(-2,5)$ everywhere
- **Answer Key:** **A** ($(-2,1)$ and $(3,5)$)

**Explanation:**
A function $f$ is increasing on an interval where its derivative $f'(x)$ is positive (i.e., $f'(x) > 0$). The problem states that $f'(x)$ lies above the $x$-axis on $(-2,1)$ and $(3,5)$, which means $f'(x) > 0$ on these intervals.

## dost1001-0962

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** Evaluate $\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{1-\cos x}{x^{2}}$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $\frac{1}{2}$
  - **B:** 0
  - **C:** 1
  - **D:** Does not exist
- **Answer Key:** **A** ($\frac{1}{2}$)

**Explanation:**
This limit is an indeterminate form of type $\frac{0}{0}$, so we can apply L'Hôpital's Rule. Taking the derivative of the numerator and denominator, we get $\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{\sin x}{2x}$. This is still an indeterminate form, so we apply L'Hôpital's Rule again: $\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{\cos x}{2} = \frac{\cos 0}{2} = \frac{1}{2}$.

## dost1001-0963

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** A table for the difference quotient $[f(2+h)-f(2)]/h$ of $f(x)=x^{2}$ gives values 3.9, 3.99, 4.01, and 4.1 for $h=-0.1$, $-0.01$, $0.01$, and $0.1$. What derivative value is suggested at $x=2$?
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** 2
  - **B:** 3
  - **C:** The derivative does not exist.
  - **D:** 4
- **Answer Key:** **D** (4)

**Explanation:**
The difference quotient $\frac{f(2+h)-f(2)}{h}$ represents the average rate of change of $f(x)$ over an interval of length $h$ starting at $x=2$. As $h$ approaches $0$, the difference quotient approaches the instantaneous rate of change, which is the derivative $f'(2)$. The given values (3.9, 3.99, 4.01, 4.1) clearly approach $4$ as $h$ gets closer to $0$.

---

## dost1001-0964

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** Which statement is correct for $f(x)=|x|$ at $x=0$?
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $f$ is differentiable but not continuous at $x=0$.
  - **B:** $f$ is both continuous and differentiable at $x=0$.
  - **C:** $f$ is continuous but not differentiable at $x=0$.
  - **D:** $f$ is neither continuous nor differentiable at $x=0$.
- **Answer Key:** **C** ($f$ is continuous but not differentiable at $x=0$.)

**Explanation:**
The function $f(x)=|x|$ is continuous at $x=0$ because $\lim_{x\to 0} |x| = 0 = f(0)$. However, it is not differentiable at $x=0$ because the graph has a sharp corner (a cusp) at this point, meaning the left-hand derivative ($-1$) does not equal the right-hand derivative ($1$).

---

## dost1001-0965

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** For the circle $x^{2}+y^{2}=25$, find $dy/dx$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=\frac{-x}{y}$
  - **B:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=\frac{x}{y}$
  - **C:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=\frac{-y}{x}$
  - **D:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=2x+2y$
- **Answer Key:** **A** ($\frac{dy}{dx}=\frac{-x}{y}$)

**Explanation:**
To find $dy/dx$ for $x^2+y^2=25$, we use implicit differentiation with respect to $x$. Differentiating both sides gives $2x + 2y \frac{dy}{dx} = 0$. Solving for $\frac{dy}{dx}$, we get $2y \frac{dy}{dx} = -2x$, which simplifies to $\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{-2x}{2y} = \frac{-x}{y}$.

## dost1001-0966

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** Differentiate $y=x^{2}e^{x}$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=2xe^{x}$
  - **B:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=x^{2}e^{x}$
  - **C:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=e^{x}(x^{2}+2)$
  - **D:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=e^{x}(x^{2}+2x)$
- **Answer Key:** **D** ($\frac{dy}{dx}=e^{x}(x^{2}+2x)$)

**Explanation:**
To differentiate $y=x^{2}e^{x}$, apply the product rule $(uv)' = u'v + uv'$. Let $u=x^2$ and $v=e^x$, so $u'=2x$ and $v'=e^x$. Substituting these into the product rule gives $\frac{dy}{dx} = (2x)e^x + x^2(e^x) = e^x(2x+x^2)$.

---

## dost1001-0967

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** If $f(x)=x^{4}-2x^{3}$, what is $f''(x)$?
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $12x^{2}-12x$
  - **B:** $4x^{3}-6x^{2}$
  - **C:** $12x^{2}-6x$
  - **D:** $6x^{2}-12x$
- **Answer Key:** **A** ($12x^{2}-12x$)

**Explanation:**
To find $f''(x)$, we first find the first derivative $f'(x)$ and then differentiate it again. Given $f(x)=x^{4}-2x^{3}$, the first derivative is $f'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(x^{4}-2x^{3}) = 4x^{3}-6x^{2}$. Differentiating $f'(x)$ gives $f''(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(4x^{3}-6x^{2}) = 12x^{2}-12x$.

---

## dost1001-0968

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** A rectangle is inscribed under the parabola $y=12-x^{2}$ above the $x$-axis, symmetric about the $y$-axis, with upper corners on the parabola. What is the maximum possible area?
- **Image:** `dost1001-q0968.png`
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** 32 square units
  - **B:** 24 square units
  - **C:** 36 square units
  - **D:** 48 square units
- **Answer Key:** **A** (32 square units)

**Explanation:**
Let the coordinates of the upper right corner of the rectangle be $(x,y)$. Since the rectangle is symmetric about the $y$-axis, its width is $2x$ and its height is $y$. The area $A$ is $A = (2x)y$. Since the upper corners are on the parabola $y=12-x^2$, we substitute $y$ to get $A(x) = 2x(12-x^2) = 24x - 2x^3$. To find the maximum area, we differentiate $A(x)$ and set it to zero: $A'(x) = 24 - 6x^2 = 0$, which gives $6x^2 = 24$, so $x^2 = 4$, and $x=2$ (since $x>0$). Substituting $x=2$ back into the area formula gives $A(2) = 2(2)(12-2^2) = 4(12-4) = 4(8) = 32$ square units.

---

## dost1001-0969

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** A 10-ft ladder leans against a wall. Its bottom slides away from the wall at 2 ft/s. How fast is the top sliding down when the bottom is 6 ft from the wall?
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** 0.75 ft/s downward
  - **B:** 2 ft/s downward
  - **C:** 3 ft/s downward
  - **D:** 1.5 ft/s downward
- **Answer Key:** **D** (1.5 ft/s downward)

**Explanation:**
Let $x$ be the distance of the bottom of the ladder from the wall, and $y$ be the height of the top of the ladder on the wall. The ladder forms a right triangle with the wall and the ground, so $x^2 + y^2 = 10^2 = 100$. We are given $\frac{dx}{dt} = 2$ ft/s and we need to find $\frac{dy}{dt}$ when $x=6$ ft. First, find $y$ when $x=6$: $6^2 + y^2 = 100 \Rightarrow 36 + y^2 = 100 \Rightarrow y^2 = 64 \Rightarrow y=8$ ft. Differentiate the equation $x^2 + y^2 = 100$ with respect to time $t$: $2x\frac{dx}{dt} + 2y\frac{dy}{dt} = 0$. Substitute the known values: $2(6)(2) + 2(8)\frac{dy}{dt} = 0 \Rightarrow 24 + 16\frac{dy}{dt} = 0 \Rightarrow 16\frac{dy}{dt} = -24 \Rightarrow \frac{dy}{dt} = -\frac{24}{16} = -\frac{3}{2} = -1.5$ ft/s. The negative sign indicates the top is sliding down, so the speed is $1.5$ ft/s downward.

---

## dost1001-0970

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** For $f(x)=x^{3}-6x^{2}+9x$, on which intervals is $f$ increasing?
- **Image:** `dost1001-q0970.png`
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $(1,3)$ only
  - **B:** $(-\infty,3)$
  - **C:** $(-\infty,1)$ and $(3,\infty)$
  - **D:** $(1,\infty)$
- **Answer Key:** **C** ($(-\infty,1)$ and $(3,\infty)$)

**Explanation:**
A function is increasing where its first derivative is positive. First, find the derivative of $f(x)=x^{3}-6x^{2}+9x$: $f'(x) = 3x^2 - 12x + 9$. Set $f'(x)=0$ to find critical points: $3x^2 - 12x + 9 = 0 \Rightarrow 3(x^2 - 4x + 3) = 0 \Rightarrow 3(x-1)(x-3) = 0$. The critical points are $x=1$ and $x=3$. Test intervals $(-\infty, 1)$, $(1, 3)$, and $(3, \infty)$. For $(-\infty, 1)$, choose $x=0$: $f'(0) = 9 > 0$, so $f$ is increasing. For $(1, 3)$, choose $x=2$: $f'(2) = 3(2^2) - 12(2) + 9 = 12 - 24 + 9 = -3 < 0$, so $f$ is decreasing. For $(3, \infty)$, choose $x=4$: $f'(4) = 3(4^2) - 12(4) + 9 = 48 - 48 + 9 = 9 > 0$, so $f$ is increasing. Thus, $f$ is increasing on $(-\infty,1)$ and $(3,\infty)$.

---

## dost1001-0971

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** For $f(x)=x^{4}-4x^{2}$, where is the graph concave downward?
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $\left(-\infty,-\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\right)\cup\left(\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}},\infty\right)$
  - **B:** $\left(-\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}},\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\right)$
  - **C:** $(-\sqrt{2},\sqrt{2})$
  - **D:** Nowhere
- **Answer Key:** **B** ($\left(-\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}},\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\right)$)

**Explanation:**
A graph is concave downward where its second derivative is negative. First, find the first derivative of $f(x)=x^{4}-4x^{2}$: $f'(x) = 4x^3 - 8x$. Then, find the second derivative: $f''(x) = 12x^2 - 8$. Set $f''(x)=0$ to find possible inflection points: $12x^2 - 8 = 0 \Rightarrow 12x^2 = 8 \Rightarrow x^2 = \frac{8}{12} = \frac{2}{3} \Rightarrow x = \pm\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}$. Test intervals $\left(-\infty, -\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\right)$, $\left(-\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}, \sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\right)$, and $\left(\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}, \infty\right)$. For $\left(-\infty, -\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\right)$, choose $x=-1$: $f''(-1) = 12(-1)^2 - 8 = 12 - 8 = 4 > 0$, so concave upward. For $\left(-\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}, \sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\right)$, choose $x=0$: $f''(0) = 12(0)^2 - 8 = -8 < 0$, so concave downward. For $\left(\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}, \infty\right)$, choose $x=1$: $f''(1) = 12(1)^2 - 8 = 4 > 0$, so concave upward. Therefore, the graph is concave downward on $\left(-\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}},\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\right)$.

---

## dost1001-0972

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** Differentiate $y=x\ln x$ for $x>0$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=\frac{1}{x}$
  - **B:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=\frac{x}{x}$
  - **C:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=\ln x+1$
  - **D:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=x\ln x+1$
- **Answer Key:** **C** ($\frac{dy}{dx}=\ln x+1$)

**Explanation:**
To differentiate $y=x\ln x$, apply the product rule $(uv)' = u'v + uv'$. Let $u=x$ and $v=\ln x$. Then $u'=\frac{d}{dx}(x)=1$ and $v'=\frac{d}{dx}(\ln x)=\frac{1}{x}$. Substituting these into the product rule gives $\frac{dy}{dx} = (1)(\ln x) + (x)\left(\frac{1}{x}\right) = \ln x + 1$.

---

## dost1001-0973

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** Differentiate $y=\ln(\sin x)$ where $\sin x>0$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=\tan x$
  - **B:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=\csc x$
  - **C:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=\cos x$
  - **D:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=\cot x$
- **Answer Key:** **D** ($\frac{dy}{dx}=\cot x$)

**Explanation:**
To differentiate $y=\ln(\sin x)$, apply the chain rule $\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{d}{du}(\ln u) \cdot \frac{du}{dx}$, where $u=\sin x$. The derivative of $\ln u$ with respect to $u$ is $\frac{1}{u}$, and the derivative of $\sin x$ with respect to $x$ is $\cos x$. Therefore, $\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sin x} \cdot \cos x = \frac{\cos x}{\sin x} = \cot x$.

---

## dost1001-0974

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** Find $\int \frac{2x}{x^{2}+1}\,dx$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $2\ln(x^{2}+1)+C$
  - **B:** $\ln(x^{2}+1)+C$
  - **C:** $\frac{1}{x^{2}+1}+C$
  - **D:** $x^{\frac{2}{x^{2}+1}}+C$
- **Answer Key:** **B** ($\ln(x^{2}+1)+C$)

**Explanation:**
This integral can be solved using $u$-substitution. Let $u = x^2+1$, then $du = 2x\,dx$. The integral becomes $\int \frac{1}{u}\,du = \ln|u|+C$. Substituting back $u = x^2+1$, we get $\ln(x^2+1)+C$.

---

## dost1001-0975

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** Find $\int \cos(2x)\,dx$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\sin(2x)+C$
  - **B:** $2\sin(2x)+C$
  - **C:** $\sin(2x)+C$
  - **D:** $-\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\sin(2x)+C$
- **Answer Key:** **A** ($\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\sin(2x)+C$)

**Explanation:**
To integrate $\cos(2x)$, we use a $u$-substitution. Let $u = 2x$, so $du = 2\,dx$, which means $dx = \frac{1}{2}\,du$. The integral becomes $\int \cos(u) \left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\,du = \frac{1}{2}\int \cos(u)\,du = \frac{1}{2}\sin(u)+C$. Substituting back $u=2x$, the result is $\frac{1}{2}\sin(2x)+C$.

---

## dost1001-0976

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** Find $\int x e^{x^{2}}\,dx$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $e^{x^{2}}+C$
  - **B:** $x^{2}e^{x^{2}}+C$
  - **C:** $\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)x^{2}e^{x^{2}}+C$
  - **D:** $\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)e^{x^{2}}+C$
- **Answer Key:** **D** ($\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)e^{x^{2}}+C$)

**Explanation:**
This integral can be solved using $u$-substitution. Let $u = x^2$, then $du = 2x\,dx$, which implies $x\,dx = \frac{1}{2}\,du$. The integral becomes $\int e^u \left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\,du = \frac{1}{2}\int e^u\,du = \frac{1}{2}e^u+C$. Substituting back $u = x^2$, we get $\frac{1}{2}e^{x^2}+C$.

---

## dost1001-0977

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** Evaluate $\int_{-1}^{1}(x^{2}+1)\,dx$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $\frac{8}{3}$
  - **B:** 2
  - **C:** $\frac{4}{3}$
  - **D:** $\frac{10}{3}$
- **Answer Key:** **A** ($\frac{8}{3}$)

**Explanation:**
First, find the antiderivative of $x^2+1$, which is $\frac{x^3}{3}+x$. Then, evaluate this antiderivative at the limits of integration: $\left(\frac{1^3}{3}+1\right) - \left(\frac{(-1)^3}{3}+(-1)\right) = \left(\frac{1}{3}+1\right) - \left(-\frac{1}{3}-1\right) = \frac{4}{3} - \left(-\frac{4}{3}\right) = \frac{4}{3} + \frac{4}{3} = \frac{8}{3}$.

---

## dost1001-0978

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** What is the area between $y=x$ and $y=x^{2}$ on $0\le x\le 1$?
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $\frac{1}{3}$
  - **B:** $\frac{1}{2}$
  - **C:** $\frac{2}{3}$
  - **D:** $\frac{1}{6}$
- **Answer Key:** **D** ($\frac{1}{6}$)

**Explanation:**
The area between two curves $f(x)$ and $g(x)$ over an interval $[a,b]$ is given by $\int_a^b |f(x)-g(x)|\,dx$. In the interval $0 \le x \le 1$, $x \ge x^2$, so the area is $\int_0^1 (x-x^2)\,dx$. Evaluating the integral: $\left[\frac{x^2}{2}-\frac{x^3}{3}\right]_0^1 = \left(\frac{1^2}{2}-\frac{1^3}{3}\right) - \left(\frac{0^2}{2}-\frac{0^3}{3}\right) = \frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{3} = \frac{3-2}{6} = \frac{1}{6}$.

---

## dost1001-0979

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** Evaluate $\int_{0}^{4}|x-2|\,dx$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** 4
  - **B:** 0
  - **C:** 2
  - **D:** 8
- **Answer Key:** **A** (4)

**Explanation:**
The absolute value function $|x-2|$ changes its definition at $x=2$. For $0 \le x < 2$, $|x-2| = -(x-2) = 2-x$, and for $2 \le x \le 4$, $|x-2| = x-2$. We split the integral: $\int_0^2 (2-x)\,dx + \int_2^4 (x-2)\,dx$. Evaluating each part: $\left[2x-\frac{x^2}{2}\right]_0^2 + \left[\frac{x^2}{2}-2x\right]_2^4 = \left((4-2)-(0)\right) + \left((8-8)-(2-4)\right) = 2 + (0-(-2)) = 2+2=4$.

---

## dost1001-0980

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** What is the average value of $f(x)=x^{2}$ on $[0,3]$?
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** 3
  - **B:** 1
  - **C:** $\frac{3}{2}$
  - **D:** 9
- **Answer Key:** **A** (3)

**Explanation:**
The average value of a function $f(x)$ on an interval $[a,b]$ is given by $\frac{1}{b-a}\int_a^b f(x)\,dx$. For $f(x)=x^2$ on $[0,3]$, the average value is $\frac{1}{3-0}\int_0^3 x^2\,dx$. Evaluating the integral: $\frac{1}{3}\left[\frac{x^3}{3}\right]_0^3 = \frac{1}{3}\left(\frac{3^3}{3}-\frac{0^3}{3}\right) = \frac{1}{3}\left(\frac{27}{3}-0\right) = \frac{1}{3}(9) = 3$.

---

## dost1001-0981

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** A particle has velocity $v(t)=6t-2t^{2}$ m/s for $0\le t\le 3$. What is its displacement over the interval?
- **Image:** `dost1001-q0981.png`
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** 6 m
  - **B:** 9 m
  - **C:** 12 m
  - **D:** 18 m
- **Answer Key:** **B** (9 m)

**Explanation:**
Displacement is the definite integral of velocity over the given time interval. So, we need to evaluate $\int_0^3 (6t-2t^2)\,dt$. The antiderivative of $6t-2t^2$ is $3t^2-\frac{2t^3}{3}$. Evaluating at the limits: $\left[3t^2-\frac{2t^3}{3}\right]_0^3 = \left(3(3)^2-\frac{2(3)^3}{3}\right) - \left(3(0)^2-\frac{2(0)^3}{3}\right) = \left(3(9)-\frac{2(27)}{3}\right) - (0) = (27-18) = 9$ m.

---

## dost1001-0982

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** Water enters a tank at rate $R(t)=5+2t$ liters/min for $0\le t\le 4$. How much water is added during the 4 minutes?
- **Image:** `dost1001-q0982.png`
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** 20 L
  - **B:** 28 L
  - **C:** 36 L
  - **D:** 44 L
- **Answer Key:** **C** (36 L)

**Explanation:**
The total amount of water added is the definite integral of the rate function $R(t)$ over the given time interval $[0, 4]$. We calculate $\int_{0}^{4} (5+2t) dt = [5t+t^2]_{0}^{4} = (5(4)+4^2) - (5(0)+0^2) = (20+16) - 0 = 36$ liters.

---

## dost1001-0983

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** A graph of $f$ has labeled points A, B, C, and D with function values $f(A)=1$, $f(B)=5$, $f(C)=3$, and $f(D)=4$. The graph is shown on a closed interval, and B is the highest point on the graph. Where is the absolute maximum?
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** At A
  - **B:** At C
  - **C:** At B
  - **D:** At D
- **Answer Key:** **C** (At B)

**Explanation:**
The absolute maximum of a function on a closed interval is the largest function value over that interval. Given that B is the highest point on the graph, its function value $f(B)=5$ must be the largest among all points, including the labeled points A, C, and D.

## dost1001-0984

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** A graph of $f$ crosses the $x$-axis at $x=2$ from positive values to negative values. What does this imply about $f$ at $x=2$?
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $f$ has a local minimum.
  - **B:** $f$ has an inflection point only.
  - **C:** $f$ is discontinuous.
  - **D:** $f$ has a local maximum.
- **Answer Key:** **D** ($f$ has a local maximum.)

**Explanation:**
If a continuous function $f$ crosses the $x$-axis at $x=2$ from positive values to negative values, it means that $f(x)>0$ for $x<2$ (and close to 2) and $f(x)<0$ for $x>2$ (and close to 2). This change in sign of $f(x)$ indicates that $f(x)$ reaches its highest point (a local maximum) at $x=2$ before decreasing.

---

## dost1001-0985

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** A sign chart for $f'$ shows $f'>0$ on $(-\infty,-1)$, $f'<0$ on $(-1,2)$, and $f'>0$ on $(2,\infty)$. Which conclusion is correct?
- **Image:** `dost1001-q0985.png`
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $f$ has local minima at both $-1$ and 2.
  - **B:** $f$ has a local maximum at $x=-1$ and a local minimum at $x=2$.
  - **C:** $f$ has local maxima at both $-1$ and 2.
  - **D:** $f$ has no local extrema.
- **Answer Key:** **B** ($f$ has a local maximum at $x=-1$ and a local minimum at $x=2$.)

**Explanation:**
According to the First Derivative Test, if $f'$ changes from positive to negative at a critical point $c$, then $f$ has a local maximum at $c$. If $f'$ changes from negative to positive at $c$, then $f$ has a local minimum at $c$. Here, $f'$ changes from positive to negative at $x=-1$ (local maximum) and from negative to positive at $x=2$ (local minimum).

---

## dost1001-0986

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** Evaluate $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{3x^{2}+1}{x^{2}-5x}$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** 0
  - **B:** 1
  - **C:** $\infty$
  - **D:** 3
- **Answer Key:** **D** (3)

**Explanation:**
To evaluate the limit of a rational function as $x \to \infty$, divide both the numerator and the denominator by the highest power of $x$ in the denominator, which is $x^2$. This yields $\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{3x^{2}/x^{2}+1/x^{2}}{x^{2}/x^{2}-5x/x^{2}} = \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{3+1/x^{2}}{1-5/x}$. As $x \to \infty$, $1/x^2 \to 0$ and $5/x \to 0$, so the limit becomes $\frac{3+0}{1-0} = 3$.

---

## dost1001-0987

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** Evaluate $\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{\ln(1+x)-x}{x^{2}}$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** 0
  - **B:** $-\frac{1}{2}$
  - **C:** $\frac{1}{2}$
  - **D:** $-1$
- **Answer Key:** **B** ($-\frac{1}{2}$)

**Explanation:**
This limit is of the indeterminate form $\frac{0}{0}$ as $x \to 0$, so L'Hôpital's Rule can be applied. Differentiating the numerator and denominator with respect to $x$ gives $\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{\frac{1}{1+x}-1}{2x}$. This is still $\frac{0}{0}$, so apply L'Hôpital's Rule again: $\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{-\frac{1}{(1+x)^2}}{2} = \frac{-1}{2}$.

---

## dost1001-0988

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** Let $f(x)=x^{2}$ for $x\le 1$ and $f(x)=ax+b$ for $x>1$. Which values of $a$ and $b$ make $f$ both continuous and differentiable at $x=1$?
- **Image:** `dost1001-q0988.png`
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $a=1$, $b=0$
  - **B:** $a=2$, $b=1$
  - **C:** $a=1$, $b=1$
  - **D:** $a=2$, $b=-1$
- **Answer Key:** **D** ($a=2$, $b=-1$)

**Explanation:**
For continuity at $x=1$, the two pieces of the function must meet: $1^2 = a(1)+b \implies 1 = a+b$. For differentiability at $x=1$, the derivatives of the two pieces must be equal at $x=1$. The derivative of $x^2$ is $2x$, and the derivative of $ax+b$ is $a$. So, $2(1) = a \implies a=2$. Substituting $a=2$ into the continuity equation gives $1 = 2+b \implies b=-1$.

---

## dost1001-0989

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** For $x>0$, differentiate $y=x^{x}$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=x\cdot x^{x-1}$
  - **B:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=x^{x}\ln x$
  - **C:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=x^{x+1}$
  - **D:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=x^{x}(\ln x+1)$
- **Answer Key:** **D** ($\frac{dy}{dx}=x^{x}(\ln x+1)$)

**Explanation:**
To differentiate $y=x^x$, use logarithmic differentiation. Take the natural logarithm of both sides: $\ln y = \ln(x^x) = x \ln x$. Differentiate both sides with respect to $x$: $\frac{1}{y}\frac{dy}{dx} = (1)(\ln x) + (x)(\frac{1}{x}) = \ln x + 1$. Finally, multiply by $y$: $\frac{dy}{dx} = y(\ln x + 1) = x^x(\ln x + 1)$.

---

## dost1001-0990

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** For the circle $x^{2}+y^{2}=25$, find $d^{2}y/dx^{2}$ at the point $(3,4)$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $-\frac{25}{64}$
  - **B:** $-\frac{3}{4}$
  - **C:** $\frac{25}{64}$
  - **D:** $-\frac{1}{4}$
- **Answer Key:** **A** ($-\frac{25}{64}$)

**Explanation:**
Differentiate the equation $x^2+y^2=25$ implicitly with respect to $x$ to get $2x+2y\frac{dy}{dx}=0$, which simplifies to $\frac{dy}{dx}=-\frac{x}{y}$. Differentiate $\frac{dy}{dx}=-\frac{x}{y}$ again using the quotient rule to find $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = -\frac{y(1)-x(\frac{dy}{dx})}{y^2}$. Substitute $\frac{dy}{dx}=-\frac{x}{y}$ into the second derivative expression to get $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = -\frac{y-x(-\frac{x}{y})}{y^2} = -\frac{y+\frac{x^2}{y}}{y^2} = -\frac{y^2+x^2}{y^3}$. At the point $(3,4)$, substitute $x=3$ and $y=4$ into the expression for $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}$: $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = -\frac{3^2+4^2}{4^3} = -\frac{9+16}{64} = -\frac{25}{64}$.

---

## dost1001-0991

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** A closed cylinder must have volume $250\pi\ \text{cm}^{3}$. What radius and height minimize its total surface area?
- **Image:** `dost1001-q0991.png`
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $r=5$ cm, $h=10$ cm
  - **B:** $r=10$ cm, $h=2.5$ cm
  - **C:** $r=5$ cm, $h=5$ cm
  - **D:** $r=\sqrt{10}$ cm, $h=25$ cm
- **Answer Key:** **A** ($r=5$ cm, $h=10$ cm)

**Explanation:**
The volume of a cylinder is $V = \pi r^2 h = 250\pi$, so $h = \frac{250}{r^2}$. The surface area is $A = 2\pi r^2 + 2\pi r h$. Substitute $h$ into the surface area formula: $A(r) = 2\pi r^2 + 2\pi r \left(\frac{250}{r^2}\right) = 2\pi r^2 + \frac{500\pi}{r}$. To minimize $A(r)$, find its derivative with respect to $r$ and set it to zero: $A'(r) = 4\pi r - \frac{500\pi}{r^2} = 0$. This gives $4\pi r = \frac{500\pi}{r^2}$, so $4r^3 = 500$, which means $r^3 = 125$, and thus $r=5$ cm. Substitute $r=5$ back into the height equation: $h = \frac{250}{5^2} = \frac{250}{25} = 10$ cm.

---

## dost1001-0992

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** What point(s) on the parabola $y=x^{2}$ are closest to the point $(0,3)$?
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $(0,0)$
  - **B:** $\left(\pm\sqrt{\frac{5}{2}},\frac{5}{2}\right)$
  - **C:** $(\pm\sqrt{3},3)$
  - **D:** $(\pm 1,1)$
- **Answer Key:** **B** ($\left(\pm\sqrt{\frac{5}{2}},\frac{5}{2}\right)$)

**Explanation:**
Let $(x,y)$ be a point on the parabola $y=x^2$. The distance squared from $(x,y)$ to $(0,3)$ is $D^2 = (x-0)^2 + (y-3)^2 = x^2 + (y-3)^2$. Substitute $x^2=y$ into the distance squared formula: $D^2(y) = y + (y-3)^2 = y + y^2 - 6y + 9 = y^2 - 5y + 9$. To minimize $D^2(y)$, find its derivative with respect to $y$ and set it to zero: $\frac{d(D^2)}{dy} = 2y - 5 = 0$, which gives $y = \frac{5}{2}$. Substitute $y=\frac{5}{2}$ back into the parabola equation $y=x^2$: $x^2 = \frac{5}{2}$, so $x = \pm\sqrt{\frac{5}{2}}$. Thus, the points are $\left(\pm\sqrt{\frac{5}{2}},\frac{5}{2}\right)$.

---

## dost1001-0993

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** Water is poured into a conical tank of height 12 cm and top radius 4 cm at $2\pi\ \text{cm}^{3}/\text{s}$. When the water depth is 3 cm, how fast is the water depth increasing?
- **Image:** `dost1001-q0993.png`
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** 2 cm/s
  - **B:** $\frac{2}{3}$ cm/s
  - **C:** $\frac{3}{2}$ cm/s
  - **D:** 6 cm/s
- **Answer Key:** **A** (2 cm/s)

**Explanation:**
For a conical tank, the ratio of radius to height is constant: $\frac{r}{h} = \frac{4}{12} = \frac{1}{3}$, so $r = \frac{h}{3}$. The volume of water in the cone is $V = \frac{1}{3}\pi r^2 h$. Substitute $r=\frac{h}{3}$ into the volume formula: $V = \frac{1}{3}\pi \left(\frac{h}{3}\right)^2 h = \frac{1}{3}\pi \frac{h^2}{9} h = \frac{\pi}{27}h^3$. Differentiate $V$ with respect to time $t$: $\frac{dV}{dt} = \frac{\pi}{27} (3h^2) \frac{dh}{dt} = \frac{\pi}{9}h^2 \frac{dh}{dt}$. We are given $\frac{dV}{dt} = 2\pi\ \text{cm}^3/\text{s}$ and $h=3\ \text{cm}$. Substitute these values: $2\pi = \frac{\pi}{9}(3^2)\frac{dh}{dt} = \frac{\pi}{9}(9)\frac{dh}{dt} = \pi\frac{dh}{dt}$. Solving for $\frac{dh}{dt}$ gives $\frac{dh}{dt} = \frac{2\pi}{\pi} = 2\ \text{cm/s}$.

## dost1001-0994

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** Differentiate $y=e^{x}\sin x$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=e^{x}\cos x$
  - **B:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=e^{x}(\sin x-\cos x)$
  - **C:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=e^{x}(\sin x+\cos x)$
  - **D:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=e^{x}\sin x\cos x$
- **Answer Key:** **C** ($\frac{dy}{dx}=e^{x}(\sin x+\cos x)$)

**Explanation:**
To differentiate $y=e^x\sin x$, apply the product rule, which states that if $y=uv$, then $\frac{dy}{dx} = u'v + uv'$. Here, let $u=e^x$ and $v=\sin x$. Then $u'=e^x$ and $v'=\cos x$. Substituting these into the product rule formula gives $\frac{dy}{dx} = (e^x)(\sin x) + (e^x)(\cos x)$. Factor out $e^x$ to get $\frac{dy}{dx} = e^x(\sin x + \cos x)$.

## dost1001-0995

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** For $x>0$, differentiate $y=x^{\sin x}$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=\sin x\cdot x^{\sin x-1}$
  - **B:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=x^{\sin x}\cos x$
  - **C:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=x^{\sin x}\left[\sin x\cdot\ln x+\frac{\cos x}{x}\right]$
  - **D:** $\frac{dy}{dx}=x^{\sin x}\left[\cos x\cdot\ln x+(\sin x)/x\right]$
- **Answer Key:** **D** ($\frac{dy}{dx}=x^{\sin x}\left[\cos x\cdot\ln x+(\sin x)/x\right]$)

**Explanation:**
To differentiate $y=x^{\sin x}$, use logarithmic differentiation. Take the natural logarithm of both sides: $\ln y = \ln(x^{\sin x}) = (\sin x)(\ln x)$. Differentiate both sides with respect to $x$: $\frac{1}{y}\frac{dy}{dx} = (\cos x)(\ln x) + (\sin x)\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)$. Multiply both sides by $y$: $\frac{dy}{dx} = y\left[\cos x\cdot\ln x + \frac{\sin x}{x}\right]$. Substitute $y=x^{\sin x}$ back into the equation: $\frac{dy}{dx} = x^{\sin x}\left[\cos x\cdot\ln x + \frac{\sin x}{x}\right]$.

## dost1001-0996

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** Find $\int x^{2}\sqrt{x^{3}+1}\,dx$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)(x^{3}+1)^{3/2}+C$
  - **B:** $\left(\frac{2}{9}\right)(x^{3}+1)^{3/2}+C$
  - **C:** $\left(\frac{1}{3}\right)(x^{3}+1)^{3/2}+C$
  - **D:** $\left(\frac{2}{9}\right)(x^{2}+1)^{3/2}+C$
- **Answer Key:** **B** ($\left(\frac{2}{9}\right)(x^{3}+1)^{3/2}+C$)

**Explanation:**
Use a u-substitution. Let $u = x^3+1$. Then, differentiate $u$ with respect to $x$: $du = 3x^2\,dx$. This means $x^2\,dx = \frac{1}{3}\,du$. Substitute $u$ and $du$ into the integral: $\int x^2\sqrt{x^3+1}\,dx = \int \sqrt{u} \left(\frac{1}{3}\,du\right) = \frac{1}{3}\int u^{1/2}\,du$. Integrate $u^{1/2}$ using the power rule for integration: $\frac{1}{3}\left(\frac{u^{1/2+1}}{1/2+1}\right) + C = \frac{1}{3}\left(\frac{u^{3/2}}{3/2}\right) + C = \frac{1}{3}\left(\frac{2}{3}u^{3/2}\right) + C = \frac{2}{9}u^{3/2} + C$. Finally, substitute $u=x^3+1$ back into the expression: $\frac{2}{9}(x^3+1)^{3/2} + C$.

---

## dost1001-0997

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** Find $\int \frac{1}{x\ln x}\,dx$ for $x>0$, $x\neq 1$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $1/\ln x+C$
  - **B:** $\ln x+C$
  - **C:** $\ln|\ln x|+C$
  - **D:** $(\ln x)^{2}/2+C$
- **Answer Key:** **C** ($\ln|\ln x|+C$)

**Explanation:**
Use a u-substitution. Let $u = \ln x$. Then, differentiate $u$ with respect to $x$: $du = \frac{1}{x}\,dx$. Substitute $u$ and $du$ into the integral: $\int \frac{1}{x\ln x}\,dx = \int \frac{1}{\ln x} \cdot \frac{1}{x}\,dx = \int \frac{1}{u}\,du$. The integral of $\frac{1}{u}$ is $\ln|u|+C$. Substitute $u=\ln x$ back into the expression: $\ln|\ln x|+C$.

---

## dost1001-0998

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** Evaluate $\int_{0}^{1} x e^{x^{2}}\,dx$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $(e-1)/2$
  - **B:** $e-1$
  - **C:** $e/2$
  - **D:** $\frac{1}{2}$
- **Answer Key:** **A** ($(e-1)/2$)

**Explanation:**
To evaluate the integral $\int_{0}^{1} x e^{x^{2}}\,dx$, we use a u-substitution. Let $u = x^2$, so $du = 2x\,dx$, which means $x\,dx = \frac{1}{2}\,du$. When $x=0$, $u=0^2=0$, and when $x=1$, $u=1^2=1$. Substituting these into the integral gives $\int_{0}^{1} e^{u} \frac{1}{2}\,du = \frac{1}{2} [e^u]_{0}^{1} = \frac{1}{2}(e^1 - e^0) = \frac{1}{2}(e-1)$.

---

## dost1001-0999

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** Find the area enclosed by $y=2x$ and $y=x^{2}$.
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $\frac{2}{3}$
  - **B:** 2
  - **C:** $\frac{4}{3}$
  - **D:** $\frac{8}{3}$
- **Answer Key:** **C** ($\frac{4}{3}$)

**Explanation:**
To find the area enclosed by $y=2x$ and $y=x^2$, first find the intersection points by setting $2x = x^2$, which gives $x^2 - 2x = 0$, or $x(x-2)=0$. The intersection points are $x=0$ and $x=2$. The area is given by the integral of the upper function minus the lower function from $x=0$ to $x=2$: $\int_{0}^{2} (2x - x^2)\,dx$. Evaluating the integral, we get $[x^2 - \frac{x^3}{3}]_{0}^{2} = (2^2 - \frac{2^3}{3}) - (0^2 - \frac{0^3}{3}) = (4 - \frac{8}{3}) - 0 = \frac{12-8}{3} = \frac{4}{3}$.

---

## dost1001-1000

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** A company's marginal cost is $C'(x)=4+0.02x$ pesos per unit, where $x$ is the number of units already produced. By how much does total cost increase when production rises from 100 to 150 units?
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** ₱325
  - **B:** ₱250
  - **C:** ₱300
  - **D:** ₱425
- **Answer Key:** **A** (₱325)

**Explanation:**
The increase in total cost when production rises from $100$ to $150$ units is given by the definite integral of the marginal cost function from $100$ to $150$. This is $\int_{100}^{150} (4 + 0.02x)\,dx$. Evaluating the integral, we get $[4x + 0.01x^2]_{100}^{150} = (4(150) + 0.01(150)^2) - (4(100) + 0.01(100)^2) = (600 + 0.01(22500)) - (400 + 0.01(10000)) = (600 + 225) - (400 + 100) = 825 - 500 = 325$.

---

## dost1001-1001

- **Topic:** calculus | **Difficulty Level:** 3
- **Question:** A population has growth rate $P'(t)=100e^{0.1t}$ people/year and initial population $P(0)=500$. What is $P(10)$?
- **Image:** `dost1001-q1001.png`
- **Choices:**
  - **A:** $500+100(e-1)$
  - **B:** $1000e$
  - **C:** $500+1000(e-1)$
  - **D:** $1500e$
- **Answer Key:** **C** ($500+1000(e-1)$)

**Explanation:**
P(10) = P(0) + ∫[0 to 10] 100 e^(0.1t) dt = 500 + 100 [e^(0.1t) / 0.1] from 0 to 10 = 500 + 1000(e - 1).
